### 278. assistant (2026-05-30T13:35:30.902Z)

# Video Recording Best Practices — Hypotheses to Validate

**Status:** Draft / hypotheses (NOT yet measured) · **Date:** 2026-05-30

> ⚠️ **Read this first.** With one exception (static camera, §1 — grounded in ADR-004), the recommendations below are **engineering hypotheses, not measured thresholds.** They are starting assumptions about how a user should record footage for the pipeline to work well. **Each must be validated empirically** by varying it and measuring precision/recall with the eval harness (see §8). As that evidence arrives, the "recommended" values here get replaced by data-backed requirements — and feed a user-facing "how to record" guide.

---

## Why this doc exists

The hosted product's output quality depends heavily on **how the input was recorded**. We want to (1) discover which recording conditions actually move precision/recall, and (2) turn the winners into clear, user-facing **best-practice guidance** so a user knows how to record footage that produces great highlights. This doc is the living hypothesis list that the golden-set/eval loop will confirm or refute.

---

## 1. Static, fixed camera ✅ (grounded, not just a hypothesis)

**Recommendation: record with a stationary, tripod-mounted camera — do NOT pan/zoom/follow the players.**

This one is **evidence-backed**, not speculative. Per **ADR-004** (Phase-3 finding): the cheap Stage-1 player-motion heuristic was *designed for static recordings*, and on **broadcast footage where the camera tracks players**, on-screen player velocity *dips during rallies and spikes on pans/cuts/replays* — making player-motion a poor rally proxy (candidate recall collapsed). A fixed camera removes that confound: player motion on-screen reflects actual play, which is exactly what the pipeline keys on.

**Implication:** a static camera is likely the single highest-impact recording instruction we can give a user.

---

## 2. Camera placement & angle *(hypothesis)*

- **Elevated, behind the baseline, centered** on the court's long axis — the classic "umpire-high / back-of-court" view.
- **Rationale (to test):** keeps both players and the full court in frame, minimizes player-occluding-shuttle, and gives the shuttle a clean background (ceiling/wall) for the tiny-fast-object tracker (WASB/TrackNet).
- **To validate:** compare baseline-elevated vs. side-on vs. low-angle on the same rallies.

## 3. Full-court framing *(hypothesis)*

- The **entire court + a margin** should stay in frame at all times; both players always visible.
- **Rationale:** rally boundaries depend on seeing the whole exchange; cropping loses serve/clear/smash context.
- **To validate:** measure boundary error (dStart/dEnd) for tight vs. loose framing.

## 4. Resolution *(hypothesis, partially grounded)*

- **Minimum 720p; 1080p recommended; 2K/4K only if it demonstrably helps.**
- **Partial grounding:** the existing validators already require ≥ 1280×720 (`config.json` `validation.min_resolution_*`). The shuttle is tiny and fast, so detector recall plausibly rises with resolution — but higher resolution also raises per-video GPU cost (see `MONETIZATION_AUDIT.md`).
- **To validate (key cost/quality knob):** run the *same* matches at 720p/1080p/4K (downscale source) and plot shuttle-detection + rally P/R vs. resolution. **Find the knee** — the lowest resolution that saturates accuracy — and make *that* the recommended minimum. Don't assume "more is better"; assume "enough is enough" and measure where enough is.

## 5. Frame rate *(hypothesis, partially grounded)*

- **Minimum 30 fps; 50/60 fps may help** the fast shuttle.
- **Partial grounding:** validators already require ≥ 29.9 fps (`validation.min_fps`). A shuttle can exceed 400 km/h, so higher fps reduces motion blur / between-frame jumps.
- **To validate:** 30 vs. 60 fps on the same rallies; measure tracker continuity and boundary error.

## 6. Lighting & contrast *(hypothesis)*

- Even, bright lighting; avoid backlight/glare; prefer a background the shuttle contrasts against.
- **Rationale:** motion-blurred white shuttle vs. bright background/skylights is a known hard case for trajectory models.
- **To validate:** flag low-light/glare clips and compare detection recall.

## 7. Operational hygiene *(hypothesis)*

- **One match per file**, minimal pre/post dead time; **no editing, cuts, overlays, or replays** in the raw file (the scene-cut validator already penalizes cuts); stable mount (no shake); consistent codec (mind the codec/patent notes in `MONETIZATION_AUDIT.md` — prefer recording in a widely-decodable format, deliver royalty-free output).

---

## 8. Validation plan (turns hypotheses → requirements)

This doc is only useful if it becomes **evidence**. Method, reusing the golden-set + eval loop:

1. Assemble a small **controlled corpus**: the same rallies recorded (or re-encoded) across each variable — static-vs-panning, resolutions, fps, angles, lighting.
2. Get golden `[start,end)` labels (see `GOLDEN_SET_VENDORING.md`).
3. Run the production pipeline; score with `metrics.py` (P/R/F1, mIoU, dStart/dEnd) **per condition**.
4. Rank variables by **impact on accuracy** and **cost** (GPU $/video scales with resolution/fps).
5. Promote the winners into a **user-facing "How to record" checklist**, with each rule traceable to a measured delta — not a guess.

**Until then, treat §§2–7 as defaults to test, not promises.** §1 (static camera) is the one we can already recommend with confidence.

### 283. user (2026-05-30T13:37:47.028Z)

1	# Golden-Set Generation & Vendor Process (v1)
2	
3	**Status:** Design v1 · **Date:** 2026-05-30
4	**Owner decisions captured:** licensed/owned video only · rally `[start,end)` depth · tiered turnaround (fast automated + slow human) · vendor bake-off · **Bangalore/India-local vendor preference**
5	
6	This document defines how we produce **golden-set ground truth** (hand-verified rally boundaries) for the badminton highlights product, how we **select annotation vendors**, and how that same machinery powers **on-demand / surprise regression testing** of the production tool.
7	
8	> **Companion docs:** the scoring mechanics live in [EVALUATION.md](EVALUATION.md); the annotated-corpus rationale is [ADR-002](DECISIONS.md); the decision record for *this* process is **ADR-006**. Video-capture quality requirements (a dependency of trustworthy golden sets) are tracked separately in [VIDEO_RECORDING_GUIDELINES.md](VIDEO_RECORDING_GUIDELINES.md).
9	
10	---
11	
12	## 1. The keystone insight: one scorer, three jobs
13	
14	A vendor's golden-set output is a list of rally `[start,end)` intervals — **structurally identical to a prediction** from our pipeline. So the *exact same* deterministic scorer in `backend/eval/metrics.py` (`interval_iou` → `match_intervals` → `score`) grades all three of:
15	
16	1. **Product vs. ground truth** — the precision/recall/F1/IoU we already report (`EVALUATION.md`).
17	2. **Vendor vs. our internal answer key** — the quality gate during vendor selection.
18	3. **Annotator vs. annotator** — inter-annotator agreement (IAA), our proxy for "is this even a well-defined task?"
19	
20	This symmetry is what makes the whole system small, auditable, and extensible. **No new scoring code is needed** — only a new *source* of intervals (the vendor).
21	
22	---
23	
24	## 2. Why this also solves the privacy nuance
25	
26	The monetization goal (hosted API) carries a "someone views the user's video" concern. This process sidesteps it **by construction**:
27	
28	- **Golden sets are built only from footage we license or own** — starting with the founder's **constantly-growing personal collection** of match videos, plus ShuttleSet (ADR-003) and any purpose-recorded clips. **No end-user uploads ever flow to a vendor.**
29	- Regression tests therefore run against a **representative held-out corpus**, not literal user content.
30	- All video egress to any third party passes through a **single provider boundary** (§4), so the security controls (DPA, no-reuse, delete-on-delivery, access logging) live in exactly one place.
31	
32	> **Honest tradeoff:** a licensed/owned corpus may not perfectly mirror real users' cameras, courts, and lighting. A green regression on our corpus is therefore *strong evidence*, not a *guarantee*, for production traffic. Revisit if field behavior diverges from corpus behavior — and see VIDEO_RECORDING_GUIDELINES.md, which aims to shrink that gap by steering users toward capture conditions our corpus represents.
33	
34	---
35	
36	## 3. Selecting vendors — the bake-off
37	
38	### Step 0 — Write the annotation guideline (highest-leverage artifact)
39	Before contacting anyone, write a precise, example-rich definition of **what a rally is**:
40	- **Start trigger:** e.g. first serve motion vs. shuttle leaving the racket — pick one, show clips.
41	- **End trigger:** shuttle hits floor/net/out, or umpire call.
42	- **Edge cases:** lets/replays, service faults, between-point walking, coaching pauses, mid-rally net-cord, broadcast replays and graphics.
43	
44	Rally boundaries are genuinely subjective; **ambiguity here is the #1 cause of bad golden sets.** This guideline belongs conceptually with the per-sport `SportProfile`, so it generalizes to tennis/cricket later.
45	
46	### Step 1 — Build a small internal gold key
47	One trusted expert (initially: the founder) hand-annotates **5–10 licensed clips** to near-perfect. This is the answer key vendors are graded against — kept private during the bake-off.
48	
49	### Step 2 — Run the bake-off (parallel pilot)
50	Send the **same** pilot clips to **2–3 shortlisted vendors** (see §6, favoring Bangalore/India), withholding the key. Grade every submission with **our own harness**:
51	
52	| Dimension | How we measure it | Tool |
53	|---|---|---|
54	| **Accuracy** | vendor CSV vs. gold key — P/R/F1, mIoU, boundary error | `eval.score()` |
55	| **Consistency** | 2 annotators per vendor → pairwise IoU (IAA) | `interval_iou` |
56	| **Cost** | quoted ₹ or $ per video-minute | vendor quote |
57	| **Turnaround** | actual vs. promised, per tier | observed |
58	| **Ops friction** | file-drop vs. API; compliance with our CSV format | `annotations.load_annotations()` |
59	| **Security** | DPA signed; DPDP/ISO posture; delete-on-delivery | contract review |
60	
61	### Step 3 — Decide on measured quality-per-dollar
62	The bake-off numbers *are* the decision — not sales decks. Low IAA at a vendor means either a weak vendor **or** a vague guideline (Step 0); investigate before blaming the vendor.
63	
64	### Step 4 — Onboard the winner(s)
65	Sign DPA + the security controls in §4; codify the guideline as their spec; agree SLAs for both tiers (§5).
66	
67	---
68	
69	## 4. The extensible architecture — an `AnnotationProvider` registry
70	
71	This codebase already uses pluggable registries (validators, segmenters, AI providers, sports). The idiomatic move is the same shape for annotation sourcing:
72	
73	```python
74	class AnnotationProvider(ABC):
75	    def submit(self, video_ref, guideline, sport) -> str:        # returns job_id
76	        ...
77	    def poll(self, job_id) -> str:                               # queued|running|done|failed
78	        ...
79	    def fetch(self, job_id) -> List[Interval]:                   # canonical [(start,end), ...]
80	        ...
81	```
82	
83	`fetch()` returns the **same `List[Interval]`** that `annotations.load_annotations()` already produces, so everything downstream is unchanged.
84	
85	Three implementations cover today plus both tiers:
86	
87	| Provider | Role | Tier |
88	|---|---|---|
89	| `FileProvider` | Reads a pre-made CSV (formalizes today's manual / ShuttleSet flow) | — |
90	| `HumanVendorProvider` | The bake-off winner; authoritative ground truth, corpus growth, deep audits | **Slow (days)** |
91	| `AutomatedProvider` | An independent strong model labels rallies programmatically | **Fast (hours)** |
92	
93	The tiers are just two providers behind one interface, selectable per call.
94	
95	---
96	
97	## 5. The regression automation (the explicit ask)
98	
99	> *"given a video, get a golden set created and verify its precision and recall against the production tool"*
100	
101	This becomes a thin wrapper over machinery that **already exists**:
102	
103	```python
104	def regress(video, tier="fast", tolerance=0.05):
105	    gt          = annotation_registry.get(tier).fetch(            # NEW: the provider
106	                      annotation_registry.get(tier).submit(video, GUIDELINE, sport))
107	    predictions = process(video)                                 # existing production pipeline → DB
108	    s           = eval.score(predictions, gt)                    # existing metrics.py, unchanged
109	    regressed   = (s.recall    < baseline[video].recall    - tolerance or
110	                   s.precision < baseline[video].precision - tolerance)
111	    if regressed: alert(video, s, baseline[video])
112	    return s
113	```
114	
115	Only **one new component** (the provider). `run_eval.py`, `harness.py`, and `metrics.py` are reused verbatim. Snapshot a `baseline.json` per corpus video so "regression" is a measured delta, not a vibe.
116	
117	**Triggers** (all feed the same `regress()`): CI on pipeline changes · on-demand (the "surprise" case) · scheduled cron · pre-release gate.
118	
119	### ⚠️ The oracle problem (a real correctness trap)
120	The fast-tier `AutomatedProvider` **must be a different model lineage than production**. If production becomes the offline ActionFormer segmenter (ADR-004) and the oracle is *also* ActionFormer, they share blind spots — the test shows green while both are wrong. Pairings that stay honest:
121	
122	- production = offline learned segmenter → fast-tier oracle = **paid Gemini full-video** (different lineage).
123	- production = Gemini hybrid → fast-tier oracle = a **different VLM** or the slow human tier.
124	
125	Treat the fast tier as a **smoke alarm**, not ground truth: any fast-tier "regression" **escalates to the slow human tier** for confirmation before it blocks a release.
126	
127	---
128	
129	## 6. Vendor shortlist (Bangalore/India-favoring)
130	
131	> **STATUS: PENDING** — a dedicated primary-source vendor research pass is running (deep-research run `wf_4f8c1c91-3a1`). This section will be filled with a verified, ranked comparison table (vendor | location | video-temporal capability | pricing model | security/DPA & DPDP posture | API/turnaround | QA process | pilot availability | verdict) and 2–3 recommended pilot candidates, **favoring vendors with a Bangalore/India delivery presence** for easier coordination, possible site visits, and data residency. Pricing is expected to be quote-only and will be flagged as unverified until a quote is in hand.
132	>
133	> Per our working agreement: this is left explicitly blank rather than filled with un-sourced vendor "facts." It will be populated only from verified research output.
134	
135	---
136	
137	## 7. Suggested phasing
138	
139	| Phase | Deliverable | Depends on |
140	|---|---|---|
141	| 0 | Annotation guideline + internal gold key (5–10 clips) | nothing — can start now |
142	| 1 | Vendor bake-off → pick winner(s) | §6 research + guideline |
143	| 2 | `AnnotationProvider` registry + `FileProvider` + `HumanVendorProvider` | winner onboarded |
144	| 3 | `AutomatedProvider` (independent oracle) + `regress()` + baseline snapshots | Phase 2 |
145	| 4 | Wire surprise triggers (CI / on-demand / scheduled) | Phase 3 |
146	
147	Phase 0 is unblocked today and is the highest-leverage step regardless of vendor choice.
148	
149	---
150	
151	## 8. Open questions
152	1. **Guideline authorship** — who owns the canonical rally definition, and is one expert's key enough, or do we want 2-expert consensus for the answer key itself?
153	2. **Corpus representativeness** — how well does the owned/licensed collection mirror eventual user capture? (Ties directly to VIDEO_RECORDING_GUIDELINES.md.)
154	3. **Baseline drift** — when production legitimately improves, how/when do we re-snapshot `baseline.json` so old baselines don't raise false regressions?
155	4. **Fast-tier oracle cost** — per-video Gemini cost at regression frequency (see MONETIZATION_AUDIT.md §4 for the per-video estimate).
156

Rostering tools. 3 Workflow Customization Alignment of video annotation tools and processes. Structured Development Milestones. Two-step production and QA annotation workflows. 4 Feedback Cycle Transparency via analytics. Real time monitoring and service delivery insights. Edge case Insights. Dynamic model improvement. 5 Evaluation Assessment of deliverable. Appraisal of key metrics, quality control processes. Model reconsideration. Analysis of business outcome. Talk to an Expert What industries need video annotation services ? Autonomous Technology & Transportation Video annotation services can help in the deployment of autonomous technology by detecting objects like sign-boards, signals, vehicles, street lights and other objects on the road. Medical AI Video annotation in healthcare helps pharmaceutical companies, device manufacturers, and healthcare provider networks to deliver quality services with the help of machine learning. Geospatial technology Video annotation tasks include extracting intelligence from satellite, aerial, and drone footage to power applications in energy, agriculture, logistics, security, and mapping. Government Video annotation is helping authorities use facial recognition to identify criminals through the use of keypoint annotation on faces taken from public footage. Manufacturing Video annotation makes manufacturing more efficient by increasing the productivity of industrial robots. Perceptive robots can help to identify defective items in production or manufacturing pitfalls. Commerce Use video annotation to understand the behaviors of consumers in stores to improve retail revenue. Analyze and track how consumers interact with shelves and use object tracking to automate mask identification. Video Annotation use cases Semantic video annotation of surveillance images iMerit’s video annotation experts have processed 500+ video datasets of various formats at a much higher level of detail than the client’s own teams. Our expert annotators solved the challenge of frame-by-frame manual processing of CCTV videos by accurately identifying and annotating required objects. Moving bounding boxes using video annotation iMerit’s video annotation experts created datasets to train the world’s most advanced machine learning solutions for rearview cameras and alert systems. Our experts annotated still and moving objects using bounding boxes in video material to identify potentially dangerous objects. Detecting operating boundaries with polyline annotation iMerit experts will comb through an entire video using the iMerit proprietary annotation platform to teach a vehicle to accurately detect street lanes, road markings, directions, divergence, and traffic. Facial recognition using keypoint annotation iMerit experts used video editing to pause and apply keypoints on a person’s face. This allowed iMerit to create world-class training datasets for use in facial recognition models. This advanced technology is helping in public areas where crimes occur to identity and apprehend perpetrators. Video annotation for computer vision and robotics Industrial robots are being taught to see with iMerit video annotation. These perceptive robots are adaptive to their environment, allowing them to respond to their environment without any human interaction. This automation is creating high production and less human accidents. Create larger harvest yields with computer vision Farmers use computer vision to monitor their crops for pests and plant diseases. Through the use of footage collected by drones, iMerit experts can teach a model to recognize pests and potential threats to crop fields by annotating the footage. Optimal athletic performance with computer vision Virtual training and home workouts can use computer vision to coach clients on form, posture, and performance. iMerit experts use video annotation to teach sports AI models how to recognize when a client is performing in a way that puts them at risk for injury or challenges their performance. VIDEO ANNOTATION IN NUMBERS 2 Million Video data points annotated 95 % Accuracy Learn more about our Video Annotation services post An Executive Guide to a Computer Vision Engagement Read More whitepaper Data Annotation For Autonomous Vehicle Development Read More Getting started! The need for speed in high-quality video annotation has never been greater. iMerit combines the best predictive and automated annotation technology with world-class data annotation experts and subject matter experts to deliver the data you need to get to production, fast. ### 296. user (2026-05-30T13:45:03.662Z)

1	# KhelAcharya Shot Intelligence Layer — Internship Execution Plan
2	
3	**Version:** 1.0  
4	**Date:** 2026-05-30  
5	**Target Duration:** 10–12 weeks (flexible)  
6	**Intern Level:** Strong final-year student or recent graduate with excellent Python + CV fundamentals
7	
8	---
9	
10	## 1. Project Vision & Success Criteria
11	
12	### Primary Goal
13	Transform the existing rally-level video indexer into a **production-grade Shot Intelligence Layer** that can:
14	- Detect individual shots within rallies/points/deliveries across multiple net/racquet sports.
15	- Classify shots with sport-specific taxonomies.
16	- Attribute shots to players.
17	- Label outcomes (winner/error/net/out) and spatial characteristics.
18	- Produce a rich, queryable event stream.
19	
20	### Strategic Context
21	This layer is the **explicit foundation** for future KhelAcharya capabilities (movement quality, fatigue detection, biomechanical/gait analysis). Everything built must be designed with extensibility in mind.
22	
23	### Definition of Done (Minimum Viable for Strong Internship)
24	By the end of the internship, the system should be able to process a full badminton or table tennis match video and answer queries such as:
25	- "Show all smashes that resulted in errors"
26	- "Compare cross-court vs down-the-line drop success rate for Player A"
27	- "List all net shots in the last 3 training sessions"
28	
29	The code must be:
30	- Well-tested with deterministic evaluation
31	- Multi-sport from the start (not badminton-only with "TODO: generalize later")
32	- Production-minded (clear schemas, migration strategy, logging, error handling)
33	
34	---
35	
36	## 2. High-Level Timeline (10–12 weeks)
37	
38	| Phase | Focus Area                              | Duration   | Key Milestone |
39	|-------|-----------------------------------------|------------|---------------|
40	| 0     | Onboarding & Design                     | Week 1     | Environment + Shot Taxonomy v1 |
41	| 1     | Data Model & Persistence                | Week 2     | Extended schema + migrations live |
42	| 2     | Shot Boundary Detection                 | Weeks 3-5  | Reliable per-shot temporal segmentation |
43	| 3     | Classification & Attribution            | Weeks 6-8  | Sport-specific shot typing + player ID |
44	| 4     | Evaluation Framework & Quality Gates    | Weeks 4-9 (parallel) | Golden shot datasets + automated scoring |
45	| 5     | Query Interface + Highlight Generation  | Weeks 9-10 | Usable CLI/API for performance queries |
46	| 6     | Multi-sport Hardening, Docs & Demo      | Weeks 11-12| 2+ sports working end-to-end + final presentation |
47	
48	---
49	
50	## 3. Detailed Phase Plans
51	
52	### Phase 0: Onboarding & Design (Week 1)
53	
54	**Objectives**
55	- Deeply understand the existing pipeline (segmentation → DB → eval).
56	- Establish shared language for "shot", "event", "outcome" across sports.
57	- Define initial shot taxonomies.
58	
59	**Deliverables**
60	1. Documented shot taxonomy for Badminton + one other sport (Table Tennis or Pickleball) in `docs/SHOT_TAXONOMY.md`.
61	2. Architecture decision record (ADR) for how shot events will relate to `play_segments`.
62	3. Local development environment fully working with at least one full match processed end-to-end.
63	
64	**Key Activities**
65	- Pair with mentor on current `yolo_hybrid` + `motion` + Gemini flow.
66	- Review existing `events` table usage in `motion.py`.
67	- Run the full evaluation harness on the golden set.
68	- Collaborative workshop: define shot types, outcomes, and spatial attributes for 2–3 sports.
69	
70	**Success Criteria**
71	- Intern can explain the full current data flow without hand-holding.
72	- First draft taxonomies approved.
73	
74	---
75	
76	### Phase 1: Data Model & Persistence (Week 2)
77	
78	**Objectives**
79	- Design a clean, extensible event model for shots.
80	- Implement schema changes with proper migrations.
81	
82	**Deliverables**
83	- Updated `Database` class with new `shot_events` table (or rich use of existing `events` table + new helper methods).
84	- `ShotEvent` dataclass / Pydantic model in a new `backend/events/` module.
85	- Migration script + test that asserts schema version.
86	- Example of inserting 50+ synthetic shot events for a segment.
87	
88	**Design Considerations (to be decided in Week 1)**
89	- Should we have a dedicated `shots` table, or keep everything in the generic `events` table?
90	- How do we represent hierarchical events (e.g., "this smash was the terminating shot of the rally")?
91	- What fields are required vs optional per sport?
92	
93	**Success Criteria**
94	- All existing tests still pass.
95	- New shot events can be queried efficiently.
96	- Schema change is documented in `DECISIONS.md`.
97	
98	---
99	
100	### Phase 2: Shot Boundary Detection (Weeks 3–5)
101	
102	**Core Technical Challenge**
103	Given a `play_segment`, detect the individual moments when shots occur.
104	
105	**Approach Options** (intern should evaluate)
106	- Pure geometric: shuttle trajectory peaks + player racket motion (using existing TrackNet/WASB output + YOLO poses).
107	- Hybrid: Use cheap motion features + small learned model or rules.
108	- Avoid heavy per-frame VLMs for production inference (cost + latency).
109	
110	**Deliverables**
111	- New module: `backend/shot_detection/` (or extension of `indexer/`).
112	- `BaseShotDetector` abstract class (following the style of `BasePlaySegmenter`).
113	- At least one working implementation for badminton that produces shot timestamps inside existing segments.
114	- Evaluation metrics for shot boundary detection (temporal IoU or boundary error against hand-labeled shots — will need annotation effort).
115	
116	**Week-by-Week**
117	- **Week 3**: Literature + code review of current motion features. Prototype using shuttle trajectory zero-crossings + velocity.
118	- **Week 4**: First working shot boundary detector integrated into the pipeline. Store as events.
119	- **Week 5**: Improve robustness (handle fast rallies, camera motion). Begin multi-sport testing.
120	
121	**Success Criteria**
122	- On a held-out set of 5–10 matches, shot detection recall > 80% with < 150ms mean boundary error (stretch: 120ms).
123	
124	---
125	
126	### Phase 3: Shot Classification & Player Attribution (Weeks 6–8)
127	
128	**Objectives**
129	- Classify each detected shot into sport-specific categories.
130	- Attribute shots to the correct player.
131	- Label outcomes (winner / unforced error / forced error / net / out / let).
132	
133	**Taxonomy Scope (minimum)**
134	- **Badminton**: Serve, Clear, Drop, Smash, Drive, Net shot, Lift, Push
135	- **Table Tennis**: Serve, Loop, Push, Block, Chop, Flick, Smash
136	- **Pickleball**: Similar to tennis + dink, lob, drive, volley, third-shot drop
137	- **Tennis** (stretch): Serve, Forehand/Backhand groundstroke, Volley, Overhead, Slice, etc.
138	
139	**Deliverables**
140	- `ShotClassifier` abstraction + at least one implementation per supported sport.
141	- Player attribution logic (leveraging existing player detection + temporal assignment).
142	- Outcome labeling (can start rule-based using landing position + rally termination signal).
143	- Integration test: full video → shots with types + players + outcomes.
144	
145	**Techniques to Explore**
146	- Geometric + kinematic features (shuttle speed, angle, player position at contact).
147	- Small supervised model (if annotated data exists by Week 6).
148	- Hybrid with occasional cheap VLM verification for training data.
149	
150	**Success Criteria**
151	- Per-shot type classification accuracy ≥ 75% on validation set (badminton first).
152	- Player attribution accuracy ≥ 90%.
153	
154	---
155	
156	### Phase 4: Evaluation Framework & Quality Gates (Parallel, Weeks 4–9)
157	
158	This is the **most important** part of the internship, matching the project's culture.
159	
160	**Deliverables**
161	- Extension of `backend/eval/` for shot-level evaluation.
162	- `ShotEvalHarness` that can score:
163	  - Shot detection (boundary accuracy)
164	  - Shot type classification (per-class precision/recall)
165	  - Outcome labeling
166	  - Player attribution
167	- Curated golden shot-annotated dataset (minimum 8–12 matches across 2 sports, with per-shot labels).
168	- PR curves, confusion matrices, and regression tests.
169	- Clear quality gates defined in `docs/EVALUATION.md` (e.g., "Do not merge if badminton smash F1 drops below X").
170	
171	**Annotation Strategy**
172	- Use existing rally annotations + new lightweight shot annotation tool (intern may need to build a simple one or use existing labeling infrastructure).
173	- Start with synthetic + semi-automated labels, then human correction.
174	
175	**Success Criteria**
176	- The project gains the ability to measure "shot intelligence quality" deterministically.
177	- Every major change has before/after numbers.
178	
179	---
180	
181	### Phase 5: Query Interface & Highlight Generation (Weeks 9–10)
182	
183	**Deliverables**
184	- `ShotQuery` interface (CLI + Python API) supporting filters like:
185	  - `shot_type=smash, outcome=error, player=A`
186	  - Natural language questions (light translation layer or structured only)
187	- Aggregate analytics (e.g., "smash success rate by court zone over last 30 days").
188	- `HighlightCompiler` that can generate video clips from shot event lists with rich metadata overlays.
189	
190	**Success Criteria**
191	- Mentor can run meaningful performance queries on real processed matches without writing SQL.
192	- Generated highlights are useful for coaching review.
193	
194	---
195	
196	### Phase 6: Multi-Sport Hardening, Documentation & Final Demo (Weeks 11–12)
197	
198	**Deliverables**
199	- At least **two sports** (Badminton + Table Tennis or Pickleball) running end-to-end with comparable quality.
200	- Comprehensive documentation:
201	  - `docs/SHOT_INTELLIGENCE_ARCHITECTURE.md`
202	  - How to add a new sport
203	  - Evaluation guide
204	- Final presentation / demo to the team (20–30 min).
205	- Clean pull request(s) with full test coverage.
206	
207	---
208	
209	## 4. Technical Constraints & Principles
210	
211	1. **Evaluation First** — No feature is complete until it has automated tests and metrics.
212	2. **Multi-sport from Day 1** — Avoid badminton-only shortcuts.
213	3. **Production Mindset** — Code must be maintainable by future engineers. Prefer clarity over cleverness.
214	4. **Permissive / Commercial Clean** — Follow the direction of the current `feat/permissive-detector-swap` work.
215	5. **Leverage Existing Assets** — Heavy reuse of `SportProfile`, segmenter registry, database, eval harness, and TrackNet/WASB outputs.
216	
217	---
218	
219	## 5. Risks & Mitigations
220	
221	| Risk | Likelihood | Mitigation |
222	|------|------------|----------|
223	| Shot boundary detection proves harder than expected due to motion blur | High | Start with geometric methods + strong evaluation early. Have fallback to denser sampling or hybrid LLM labeling for training data. |
224	| Insufficient annotated shot data | Medium | Plan annotation effort in Phase 0–1. Use synthetic data + active learning. |
225	| Scope creep into full biomechanics | Medium | Strictly scope to "shot intelligence" (what happened + when + basic classification). Deeper pose/gait is explicitly future work. |
226	| Multi-sport generalization too slow | Medium | Prioritize 1–2 sports deeply, then prove extensibility pattern with a third. |
227	
228	---
229	
230	## 6. Mentoring & Collaboration Model
231	
232	- **Weekly sync** (1 hour): Progress, blockers, design decisions.
233	- **Pair programming** on hard problems (especially evaluation and multi-sport abstraction).
234	- **Code review** on every non-trivial PR.
235	- Intern is expected to write ADRs for major technical choices (following existing `docs/DECISIONS.md` format).
236	
237	---
238	
239	## 7. Stretch Goals (if time permits)
240	
241	- Basic web UI for exploring shots in a match.
242	- Simple fatigue signal (e.g., shot speed degradation over long rallies).
243	- Export to coaching tools (CSV + video + JSON).
244	- First step toward movement quality (e.g., "smash preparation posture" signals using pose).
245	
246	---
247	
248	## 8. Next Steps After Internship
249	
250	This work should leave the project in a state where:
251	- New sports can be added with clear patterns.
252	- Shot-level data becomes the default substrate for all future analytics (including the original gait/biomechanics vision).
253	- The evaluation culture is even stronger than before.
254	
255	---
256	
257	**Document Owner:** Avi Dullu  
258	**Review Cadence:** Update at end of each major phase with the intern.
259	
260	This plan is intentionally ambitious but grounded in the existing strong engineering foundation of the badminton-highlight-indexer project. A strong intern who embraces the evaluation-first mindset will ship something genuinely valuable and portfolio-defining.

As a trusted data annotation company, Techasoft specializes in offering comprehensive services including data labeling, image recognition, and data annotation for various domains such as machine learning, artificial intelligence, autonomous vehicles, robotics, and more. Video annotation encompasses the annotation of objects, actions, or events within video data. This includes tasks such as activity recognition (labeling specific actions or activities in videos), object tracking (tracking the movement of objects over time), gesture recognition (identifying and categorizing human gestures), and scene classification (assigning labels to different scenes in videos).

**SunTec India**
With 25+ years of industry experience, SunTec India has assumed the mantle of a leading and reputed service provider in a wide variety of data, technology, and digital domains. SunTec India's video annotation services are helmed by machine learning engineers and supported by a rich set of tools, a growing array of labels, and well-documented APIs.

These companies offer comprehensive video labeling and computer vision annotation services tailored to various industries and use cases.

## Key India-Based Video Annotation Vendors

**Specialized in Temporal Video Labeling:**

Dataentry India specializes in time-series annotation—capturing actions, transitions, and event sequences across continuous video streams, with each labeled segment tagged with start and end frames, total duration, and sequence order to preserve temporal context. They train AI models for use cases where understanding how events unfold over time is essential, such as surveillance incident tagging, sports performance analysis, or manufacturing line monitoring.

**Sports Analytics Focus:**

Labeling player movements, ball trajectories, and game interactions during sports events allow AI to track player performance, movements, and team strategies, supporting advanced sports analytics.

Data-Entry-India.com offers injury and illness detection by labeling abnormal movements and behavioral patterns of players in sports footage to help AI systems detect early signs of injury or fatigue, and instant highlights generation where key events such as goals, fouls, or significant plays are annotated in videos, allowing AI to automatically identify, rank, and compile highlight reels in real time.

**Pilot Program Availability:**

Prudent Partners offers a small pilot batch of 50–100 images annotated for client review with revisions incorporated to align with quality expectations, and they offer a free pilot for qualified projects to evaluate their quality, communication, and turnaround.

Other major vendors mentioned include Srishta Technology, Infosearch BPO, SunTec India, and Data Outsourcing India, all offering video annotation services with varying levels of sports analytics and temporal annotation capabilities.

## Acquisition Overview

TELUS International acquired Bangalore-based Playment, a leader in data annotation and computer vision tools and services specialized in 2D and 3D image, video and LiDAR. The acquisition occurred in 2021.

## About Playment

Playment provides fully managed data annotation services that include AI strategy consulting and a SaaS-based platform that transforms videos and images into high-quality annotated data. Playment was founded in 2015 and received funding from Elevation Capital and Y Combinator.

## Operations and Workforce

With a focus on data security, user experience and efficiency, and by engaging India's largest on-demand skilled and secure workforce, Playment has established a hyper-productive environment that delivers datasets faster than its competitors. All 85 of the startup's employees, including founders, joined TELUS post acquisition.

## Client Base

Playment services international clients including Samsung, German automotive tools giant ZF, US-based self driving solution company Nuro and Daimler AG among others.

# Autonomous loop check

You're being invoked on a timer while the user is away or occupied. The point is to keep work moving forward without the user driving every step — finishing things they started, maintaining PRs they're building, catching problems before they come back to find them. You're a steward, not an initiator. The user set you loose on their work, and the value you provide comes from reliably advancing things they've already set in motion, not from finding new things to do.

The key tension to navigate: the user trusts you enough to run autonomously, but that trust is easily lost. Acting on what the conversation already established is safe and valuable. Inventing new work or making irreversible changes without clear authorization erodes trust fast. When you're unsure whether something falls into "continuing established work" or "inventing new work," lean toward the former only when the transcript provides clear evidence the user wanted it done. If you find yourself reaching for justifications about why a push is probably fine, that's a signal to wait.

## What to act on

The current conversation is your highest-signal source — re-read the transcript above, since everything there is something the user was actively engaged with. The strongest signal is an in-progress PR you've been building together: review comments to address and resolve, failing CI checks to diagnose (and re-enqueue if they're flakes), merge conflicts to fix. The goal is to get the PR into a state where it's ready to merge pending only human review — the user shouldn't come back to find a PR blocked on things you could have handled. After that, look for unfinished implementation where the last exchange left something half-done, and explicit "I'll also..." or "next I'll..." commitments the conversation made and didn't honor. Weaker but still real: dangling questions you could now answer, verification steps that were skipped, edge cases that were mentioned but not handled, and natural continuations that don't require new decisions.

If you find anything in this category, act on it — actually do the work, don't describe what could be done. Run the tests, don't say "you could run the tests." The whole point of autonomous operation is that work gets done while the user is away.

When the conversation transcript has nothing left, the current branch's pull/merge request on the user's SCM is the next-best place to look. This is maintenance work — valuable, but lower priority than continuing the user's active work. Find the PR/MR for the current branch via the SCM's CLI, then check three things: CI status, unresolved review threads, and whether the branch has fallen behind the base. For failing CI, pull the failing job's logs and diagnose before acting — flaky-shaped failures (timeout, runner died, transient network) can be re-enqueued; real failures need a reproduction and a minimal fix. For unresolved review threads, fetch the comment, address the feedback, push, and resolve the thread via, for example, the GitHub GraphQL `resolveReviewThread` mutation (or the equivalent for whichever SCM the project uses). Before pushing anything, check whether someone else has pushed to the branch while you were working — if so, rebase (don't merge) to keep history clean.

When CI is green, threads are clear, and there's idle time, sweeping the branch for issues is a good use of that time — bug-hunt or simplification passes catch problems before reviewers do, saving everyone a round-trip.

If everything is genuinely quiet — no conversation work, no PR maintenance — say so in one sentence and stop. No summary of what you checked, no list of what you might do later. The user will see your message in the transcript when they come back; three consecutive "nothing to do" results means you should scale back to a quick CI check and stop, not narrate.

## Repeated invocations

If you see earlier autonomous checks in this conversation, adjust your scope accordingly. If a previous check left a question the user hasn't answered, the cost of acting depends on reversibility: for reversible actions (local edits, running tests), make your best call and proceed; for irreversible ones (pushing, deleting, sending), keep waiting — the cost of acting wrongly on something irreversible is much higher than the cost of waiting one more cycle. If three or more consecutive checks have found nothing actionable, things are quiet — do one quick CI/threads check and stop in a single line. Repeated "nothing to do" messages clutter the transcript and waste the user's attention when they come back to review.

Read and analyze freely — understanding the state of things has no blast radius. Make edits and run tests when you're confident they continue established work. Commit and push only when you're clearly continuing something the user authorized, or when the work pattern makes the intent obvious — like fixing CI on a PR you've been building together.


---

# Memory Consolidation

You're doing a reflective pass over what you've learned about this user and their work. The goal: a future session should be able to orient quickly — who they work with, what they're focused on, how they like things done — without re-asking.

Your system prompt's auto-memory section defines the directory, file format, and memory types. Follow it.

## Phase 1 — Take stock

- List the memory directory and read the index (`MEMORY.md`)
- Skim each topic file. Note which ones overlap, which look stale, which are thin.

## Phase 2 — Consolidate

**Separate the durable from the dated.** Preferences, working style, key relationships, and recurring workflows are durable — keep and sharpen them. Specific projects, deadlines, and one-off tasks are dated — if the date has passed or the work is done, retire the file or fold the lasting takeaway (e.g. "user prefers X format for launch docs") into a durable one.

**Merge overlaps.** If two files describe the same person, project, or preference, combine into one and keep the richer file's path.

**Fix time references.** Convert "next week", "this quarter", "by Friday" to absolute dates so they stay readable later.

**Drop what's easy to re-find.** If a memory just restates something you could pull from the user's calendar, docs, or connected tools on demand, cut it. Keep what's hard to re-derive: stated preferences, context behind a decision, who to go to for what.

## Phase 3 — Tidy the index

Update `MEMORY.md` so it stays under 200 lines and ~25KB. One line per entry, under ~150 chars: `- [Title](file.md) — one-line hook`.

- Remove pointers to retired memories
- Shorten any line carrying detail that belongs in the topic file
- Add anything newly important

Finish with a short summary: how many files you touched and what changed.

17	`backend/database.py::_init_db`.
18	
19	**Why:** these intentions aren't derivable from the code alone.
20	**How to apply:** when extending detection, write candidates via `add_candidate_segment` with
21	a `detector` name + `stats`; preserve the annotation columns and the version-migration pattern.

1	---
2	name: collector-indexer-bridge
3	description: "Plan to feed sports-data-collector's rally annotations into the indexer to train/eval a generic temporal rally segmenter"
4	metadata: 
5	  node_type: memory
6	  type: project
7	  originSessionId: 7572cbc9-3822-461e-8779-c8cec498cde9
8	---
9	
10	Goal: use `sports-data-collector`'s commercially-licensed rally annotations to **train and evaluate a generic temporal rally segmenter** in `badminton-highlight-indexer`, reusable across sports (badminton/tennis/cricket share a `(video_id, ordinal, start_time, end_time)` shape).
11	
12	Key reframing established 2026-05-26: the indexer's `yolo_hybrid` segmenter uses **stock yolov8n (person class only) + a velocity heuristic** — there is no trained badminton model. The collector's data is **temporal-only** (rally `[start,end]`, stroke counts, scores; NO per-frame shuttle `(x,y)`). So this data trains/tunes the **temporal segmentation** stage, NOT a shuttle object detector (that needs coordinate labels → see [[tracknet-wasb-wsl2-decision]] and [[candidate-segments-finetuning]]).
13	
14	Phased plan (user picked: train a temporal segmenter; exporter lives as a collector CLI subcommand):
15	- **Phase 1 (DONE):** `python -m src.cli.curate export` in sports-data-collector. Emits `<out>/<sport>/<video_id>.csv` (indexer `start,end` format) + `manifest.json`. Filters curated + `is_commercial` by default. Code: `SportsDatabase.get_segment_intervals()` + `SEGMENT_TABLES` map in `src/core/db.py`; `export_eval_set()` in `src/cli/curate.py`.
16	- **Phase 2 (code DONE; bulk re-fetch BLOCKED on cookies):** on-disk videos were **256×144** because the crawler hard-coded `yt-dlp -f worstvideo`. Fixed: `src/core/downloader.py` always fetches best resolution (`bv*+ba/b -S res,...`, merged mp4, ffprobes actual w/h/fps, pins `youtube:player_client=web_safari`, enables EJS solver `--remote-components ejs:github`, auto-adds `~/.deno/bin` to PATH). New `curate fetch` re-downloads existing DB entries at best res, updates `local_path`/`resolution`/`fps` **in place** (rallies + curation preserved — NO clean slate). Guard: below-min-height downloads are discarded, not committed. deno 2.8.0 installed. Both repos now on GitHub under `avidullu` (badminton-highlight-indexer; sports-data-collector created PRIVATE 2026-05-26); both `master` in sync with origin.
17	  - **RESOLVED + COMPLETE (2026-05-26):** root cause of persistent 144p was the `web/web_safari` clients being forced into SABR streaming (strips download URLs); fix = `player_client=default` + authenticated Firefox Premium cookies (`--cookies-from-browser firefox`; Firefox reads cleanly on Windows, Chrome blocked by DPAPI app-bound encryption #10927). Bulk batch initially failed all-at-once from YouTube rate-limiting → added `--sleep`/`--retries`/`--retry-backoff` to fetch. Final result: **all 22 fetchable badminton matches now true 1920×1080** (verified on disk, rallies intact), DB committed. Unfetchable: shuttleset_12 (no URL in ShuttleSet catalog), my_custom_local_match + staging_match_non_comm (mock/non-commercial). ~26GB in data/videos (gitignored). ShuttleSet corpus tops out at 1080p (no 4K). **Phase 2 done.**
18	- **Phase 3 (IN PROGRESS):** eval/tune loop. Built `run_phase3_eval.py` + `backend/eval/batch.py` in indexer (walks manifest, processes each video, scores vs GT, per-video + corpus aggregate; pure helpers tested). Loop validated on 1 video with `motion`. Committed on indexer branch `feat/phase3-eval-and-offline-roadmap`. Indexer `output/` is gitignored. NOTE: indexer validators gate on min_fps 29.9 but ShuttleSet videos are 25fps — the driver calls the segmenter directly, bypassing the validation gate.
19	- **Phase 3 EVIDENCE (2026-05-26):** ran motion (shuttleset_1) + yolo_hybrid (shuttleset_8). Findings: (1) Gemini key is FREE TIER (20 req/day) → 429 quota → API path dead, pivot vindicated. (2) Processing is SLOW (~50min per 1080p video). (3) **YOLO candidate recall 0.05@IoU0.5** (0.72@0.01; 72% rallies have some overlap but median coverage ~20%); **merging candidates does NOT help**. Root cause: broadcast camera TRACKS players → player pixel-velocity is a poor rally proxy (designed for static GoPro). `motion` also useless (0 TP). → **Offline substrate decided: WASB shuttle-motion (ADR-001), NOT YOLO.** Caveat: 1 video, confirm on 2-3 more via free candidates-only YOLO pass. Already-built training-data + classifier layers (`backend/eval/training.py`, `classifier.py`, committed on branch `feat/phase3-eval-and-offline-roadmap`) are substrate-agnostic → will consume WASB windows/features.
20	- **Phase 4 (NEXT — direction set 2026-05-26):** build an OFFLINE, API-free learned segmenter (e.g. `yolo_learned`) — replace the Gemini refinement with a lightweight classifier trained on rally annotations + already-stored `candidate_segments.stats`. User explicitly wants NO external API. Substrate (YOLO player-motion features vs WASB shuttle-motion) decided by the **candidate-stage recall** from the running yolo_hybrid validation: high recall → YOLO features; low → WASB. Full paid 22-video Gemini eval NOT run (only 1 reference video). Decisions documented: indexer `docs/ROADMAP.md` + `docs/DECISIONS.md` ADR-002/003/004; collector `docs/PIPELINE_ROLE.md`.
21	
22	**Critical integration gotcha:** indexer keys videos by **file MD5**, collector by human `video_id`. Re-encoding/downloading changes bytes→MD5, so acquisition must precede processing and the manifest tracks which exact file was used.
23

### 357. user (2026-05-30T14:57:40.580Z)

<system-reminder>This memory is 4 days old. Memories are point-in-time observations, not live state — claims about code behavior or file:line citations may be outdated. Verify against current code before asserting as fact.</system-reminder>
1	---
2	name: tracknet-wasb-wsl2-decision
3	description: Why precise shuttle tracking uses self-hosted TrackNetV4/WASB via WSL2, not an LLM API
4	metadata:
5	  type: project
6	---
7	
8	For **precise moving-object (shuttlecock/ball) detection**, the plan is self-hosted **trajectory models**, NOT a multimodal LLM API. LLM APIs (Gemini/Claude) are good for *semantic* rally segmentation but cannot reliably localize a tiny, fast, motion-blurred shuttle frame-by-frame.
9	
10	Chosen models:
11	- **TrackNetV4** (TensorFlow, https://github.com/AR4152/TrackNetV4) — badminton-specific, highest reported shuttle accuracy, ships `src/predict.py` that outputs per-frame (X,Y) coordinate CSV. Primary choice.
12	- **WASB** (PyTorch, https://github.com/nttcom/WASB-SBDT) — multi-sport; fits the SportProfile abstraction. **Ships pretrained badminton weights** (`wasb_badminton_best.pth.tar` + tracknetv2/monotrack/ballseg badminton variants), so it's runnable on badminton TODAY — unlike TrackNetV4 which has NO hosted weights and must be trained first. Needs a small custom-video inference wrapper (repo only documents dataset eval). This flips sequencing: WASB is the faster path to a working baseline.
13	
14	**Setup status (done 2026-05-25):** Both run in WSL2 Ubuntu 24.04, GPU = RTX 2070 Super (8GB). Miniconda at `~/miniconda3`. Envs: `TrackNetV4` (TF 2.17, GPU detected) and `wasb` (torch 1.11+cu113, CUDA ok). Repos in `~/models/`. WASB weights in `~/models/WASB-SBDT/pretrained_weights/`. Full setup/usage in repo `docs/TRACKNET_WSL_SETUP.md`; decision in `docs/DECISIONS.md` ADR-001.
15	**TrackNetV4 gotchas:** (1) `docs/RESULT.md` weights links are placeholder `(#)` — no hosted weights; (2) `src/predict.py` `custom_objects` references unimported layers → NameError, needs a 1-line patch before loading a model.
16	
17	**Runtime decision: WSL2, not native Ubuntu.** Why: the whole indexer pipeline already lives on Windows, so WSL2 keeps one dev environment and just quarantines the TensorFlow/PyTorch envs. Native Ubuntu would mean dual-boot + migrating the entire project for marginal gain. Decided 2026-05-25.
18	**Key WSL gotcha:** `/mnt/c` (Windows drive) I/O is slow — stage match videos inside the WSL filesystem (e.g. `~/clips/`) before inference, don't read big mp4s across the boundary.
19	
20	**How to apply:** integrate via a **subprocess adapter** — a detector module beside [[candidate-segments-finetuning]] / yolo_hybrid that shells out to the WSL `predict.py`, reads the coordinate CSV, and converts trajectory → candidate rallies feeding the existing stitching flow. Keeps TF dependency isolated. User is manually annotating videos to fine-tune after integration.
21

1	---
2	name: monetization-plan
3	description: Project will be monetized as a hosted video→highlights API; dependency licensing/cost is a first-class constraint
4	metadata: 
5	  node_type: memory
6	  type: project
7	  originSessionId: aa329f09-28b7-4acc-b062-98ec7e905abc
8	---
9	
10	The long-term goal is to **monetize this project as a hosted API**: run it on a cloud machine where users upload their badminton videos and receive auto-generated highlights. Revenue depends on healthy per-video margins and on not being legally blocked.
11	
12	**Why:** This reframes dependency choices as a business constraint, not just an engineering one. Two known risks the user flagged:
13	- **Ultralytics YOLO** — AGPL-3.0; the network/SaaS clause likely makes it a blocker for a closed-source hosted service. See [[tracknet-wasb-wsl2-decision]] and [[candidate-segments-finetuning]].
14	- **Gemini API calls** — per-call cost and commercial-use/data terms may hurt margins or the monetization path.
15	
16	**How to apply:** Prefer permissively-licensed (Apache/MIT/BSD), self-hostable components over AGPL/GPL libraries and metered external APIs. **Key distinction the user made:** we will NOT distribute the code/binary — it stays server-side — so we may *host* software on the machine (e.g. ffmpeg invoked as a separate process) rather than bundling it; copyleft obligations triggered by *distribution* (GPL/LGPL) are generally not triggered by pure SaaS hosting, BUT AGPL's network clause still bites. Evaluate every new dependency against: (1) license vs. SaaS hosting, (2) per-video cost at scale, (3) self-hostability. The WASB trajectory path is the leading YOLO replacement to explore.
17

9	
10	Standing working agreement (user confirmed 2026-05-30).
11	
12	**The load-bearing rule (user called it "the silver bullet"): never attribute a claim to a physical/real entity that did not happen.** No citing a "code review," "discussion," "feedback," meeting, ticket, or source that did not occur. A false *provenance* is the bright, checkable line — if a statement carries a provenance, the provenance must be real.
13	
14	**Why this exists (grounded in this session):** I (a) recorded research conclusions *before reading the actual output* and got the WASB license backwards, and (b) created a memory file describing a feature idea falsely attributed to "code-review feedback" that never took place. Both were caught and corrected/deleted.
15	
16	**Guardrails to hold:**
17	- Anything recorded as fact — memory, docs, code comments, commit messages, PR bodies — must trace to: something the user said, something in the files, or a cited source. No source → don't write it as fact.
18	- **Never invent provenance** (the silver-bullet rule).
19	- **Read before recording** — don't write conclusions before reading the underlying output.
20	- **Unprompted ideas** (features, refactors, "wouldn't it be nice") → raise as a proposal *in conversation*; do not silently persist them to memory/docs.
21	- **Invited imagination** (brainstorm / design / draft when asked) is encouraged — just clearly flagged as a proposal, never dressed up as established fact.
22	- Keep labeling confidence honestly: "verified" vs "I think" vs "guessing."

1	---
2	name: monetization-research-status

4	metadata:

7	  originSessionId: aa329f09-28b7-4acc-b062-98ec7e905abc
8	---
9	
10	Deep-research on monetization blockers **COMPLETED & READ from the actual synthesis output** (Run `wf_ddd29371-8c2`, synth agent `a8754fe8dc7400125`). Stats: 5 angles, 15 sources, 48 claims → 25 verified (3-vote adversarial) → 24 confirmed / 1 killed → 10 findings. License findings HIGH confidence (verified vs raw LICENSE files / official terms, as of 2026-05). See [[monetization-plan]]. Full journal: `aa329f09-.../subagents/workflows/wf_ddd29371-8c2/journal.jsonl`.
11	
12	## Verdicts (verified)
13	**🔴 BLOCKERS (only two, both fixable):**
14	1. **ultralytics** — code AND bundled `yolov8n.pt` weights are **AGPL-3.0**; §13 network clause IS triggered by remote inference in a closed-source hosted API. Weights inherit the code's AGPL (maintainer statement, repo discussion #9054). Only non-replacement escape = **Ultralytics Enterprise License** (private/quote-only pricing).
15	2. **Specific model weights:** **MonoTrack** = Adobe Research License (noncommercial only) → blocker. **YOLO-NAS weights** = Deci license banning commercial/production/managed-service use → blocker. Avoid both.
16	
17	**🟡 WATCH:**
18	- **Gemini API (service):** commercial use OK; on the **PAID tier** Google does NOT train on or human-review uploaded prompts/files/video — but the **free tier does both**, so production MUST bill the API. Constraint is cost, not license. SDK `google-genai` itself = Apache-2.0 (clear).
19	- **Codec patents** (H.264/HEVC/AAC): independent of ffmpeg's software license; royalties attach to encode/decode. Mitigate by emitting **royalty-free output** (AV1 via SVT-AV1 or NVENC-AV1 on Ada GPUs; VP9; + Opus). Inbound HEVC (GoPro) decode = residual, low-but-unsettled risk.
20	
21	**🟢 CLEAR (keep):** torch/torchvision (BSD) · opencv-python (MIT pkg / Apache OpenCV / **LGPL** bundled ffmpeg) · numpy/fastapi/uvicorn/pydantic/jinja2/python-dotenv (BSD/MIT) · lapx (BSD) · google-genai SDK (Apache-2.0) · ffmpeg subprocess (LGPL default / GPL with --enable-gpl, **NOT AGPL** → no source-disclosure for server-side use).
22	
23	## ⚠️ Corrections to earlier wrong notes (do not trust prior drafts)
24	- **WASB-SBDT is MIT = CLEAR** (verified via GitHub Licenses API) — NOT non-commercial. Both TrackNetV3 repos (qaz812345, alenzenx) are also **MIT = CLEAR**. This supersedes the worry in [[tracknet-wasb-wsl2-decision]]; the shuttle path is fine to use commercially (code). The shuttle blocker is only **MonoTrack**.
25	- **Qwen2.5-VL weight licenses are tier-split:** **7B & 32B = Apache-2.0 (CLEAR)**, **3B = qwen-research (NONCOMMERCIAL — avoid)**, **72B = custom Qwen** (commercial OK below 100M MAU). InternVL2.5 = MIT.
26	
27	## Recommended permissive target stack
28	- **Detector (replace YOLOv8):** torchvision `fasterrcnn/retinanet/fcos` (BSD, zero new deps, person=COCO id 0) · OR **YOLOX** (Apache code+weights; repo stale since 2023) · OR **RT-DETRv2** (lyuwenyu/Baidu, Apache — NOT the ultralytics RT-DETR port) / **D-FINE** (Apache core; avoid its bundled AGPL YOLO subcomponent). Entry: `backend/indexer/yolo_hybrid.py`.
29	- **Shuttle tracking:** WASB-SBDT or TrackNetV3 (MIT) are usable as-is; just confirm the *pretrained-weights/dataset* terms or train own. Entry: `backend/indexer/tracknet_hybrid.py`, `backend/detectors/`.
30	- **LLM step (all 3 clear):** (A) eliminate via **ActionFormer (MIT)** offline temporal action localization (Phase 4; best margin) · (B) self-host **Qwen2.5-VL-7B/32B (Apache-2.0)** or **InternVL2.5 (MIT)** · (C) keep **Gemini 2.5 Flash paid tier**. Entry: `backend/providers/gemini.py`.
31	
32	## Costs (order-of-magnitude only)
33	Gemini 2.5 Flash: $0.30/1M input (incl. video), $2.50/1M output; video ~263 tokens/sec ⇒ ~**$0.05 input per 10-min clip** — cheap per video. Real cost driver for self-host/offline = GPU VM hours; exact A/B/C per-video comparison is an OPEN QUESTION (not yet quantified).
34	
35	## Open questions / needs-human-check (from the report's own caveats)
36	1. Actual quoted **Ultralytics Enterprise** price (to compare vs swap cost).
37	2. **Pretrained-weights/dataset** licenses for YOLOX / ActionFormer / TrackNetV3 (repos state a *code* license but don't separately license the distributed checkpoints) — train-own or confirm per-checkpoint.
38	3. Exact current **Via-LA (AVC) / Access Advance (HEVC)** royalty rates & free thresholds (Via-LA page blocked auto-fetch) — verify with counsel.
39	4. Real per-video **GPU cost/latency of Qwen2.5-VL-7B (L4/A10)** vs Gemini paid-tier at expected throughput.
40	
41	## Next step
42	Likely an **ADR** (matches ADR-002…004) recording the target stack + Ultralytics→torchvision/YOLOX migration plan. Optionally write the full report to `docs/MONETIZATION_AUDIT.md`.
43	
44	## Process note
45	Earlier in this session I twice recorded conclusions BEFORE reading the synthesis output (a status-check misfire made me think the run had failed, then I guessed findings). Both were wrong (esp. the WASB verdict). Lesson: always read `journal.jsonl` / the synth agent result before recording anything.
46

### 369. assistant (2026-05-30T15:01:23.886Z)

---
name: monetization-research-status
description: Verified outcome of the monetization dependency-licensing audit — verdicts + what's done vs still open
metadata:
  type: project
---

Deep-research dependency audit for the hosted-API path **COMPLETED & verified 2026-05-30** against primary LICENSE files / official terms. The **full cited report is version-controlled** in `docs/MONETIZATION_AUDIT.md`, and the decision is `docs/DECISIONS.md` **ADR-005**. This file keeps only the durable verdicts + live open items. See [[monetization-plan]].

## Verdicts
**🔴 Blockers (both handled in plan):**
1. **ultralytics** lib + `yolov8n.pt` weights = **AGPL-3.0**; §13 triggers source-disclosure for a closed-source hosted service. → **DONE:** swapped to torchvision Faster R-CNN + supervision ByteTrack (PR #8, merged). Paid escape would've been Ultralytics Enterprise (quote-only).
2. **Specific weights to avoid:** **MonoTrack** (Adobe, noncommercial) and **YOLO-NAS** weights (Deci, bans commercial/managed-service). Just don't use them.

**🟡 Watch:** Gemini = **paid tier mandatory** (free tier trains on + human-reviews uploads); legal-clean, cost is the concern. **Codec patents** (H.264/HEVC/AAC) attach to encode/decode independent of ffmpeg's license → emit **royalty-free AV1/VP9 + Opus**; inbound HEVC decode = low-but-unsettled residual risk.

**🟢 Clear (keep):** torch/torchvision (BSD), opencv-python (Apache; bundled ffmpeg is LGPL not GPL), numpy/fastapi/uvicorn/pydantic/jinja2/python-dotenv (BSD/MIT), supervision (MIT), google-genai SDK (Apache-2.0), ffmpeg-as-subprocess (LGPL/GPL, **not** AGPL → no disclosure for server-side use).

**Corrections that superseded earlier wrong notes:** WASB-SBDT + both TrackNetV3 repos are **MIT = CLEAR** (not non-commercial) — see [[tracknet-wasb-wsl2-decision]]. Qwen2.5-VL weights are tier-split: **7B & 32B = Apache-2.0 (use these)**, 3B = qwen-research (noncommercial, avoid), 72B = custom Qwen (OK <100M MAU); InternVL2.5 = MIT.

## Future LLM-step options (all viable, not yet chosen)
**A** eliminate via offline ActionFormer/MS-TCN (MIT, Phase-4) · **B** self-host Qwen2.5-VL-7B/32B (Apache) · **C** keep Gemini paid-tier. Rough per-video cost A <$0.05 · B $0.05–0.30 · C $0.05–0.40.

## Still open (needs offline/human action — not re-derivable)

### 372. assistant (2026-05-30T15:01:45.063Z)

---
name: golden-set-vendoring
description: Plan to generate golden-set rally labels via third-party vendors + an extensible regression-testing flow
metadata:
  type: project
---

How we produce trusted **golden-set ground truth** (hand-verified rally `[start,end)` labels) and use it to regression-test the production tool. Full design + vendor shortlist is version-controlled in `docs/GOLDEN_SET_VENDORING.md` (and ADR-006); this file holds the durable decisions.

**Keystone insight:** a vendor's label CSV is structurally identical to a pipeline prediction, so the existing `backend/eval/metrics.py` (`interval_iou`→`match_intervals`→`score`) grades all three of: product-vs-GT, vendor-vs-answer-key, and annotator-vs-annotator (IAA). No new scoring code — only a new *source* of intervals.

**Decisions locked with the user (2026-05-30):**
- **Licensed/owned video only** — vendors never see user uploads (this is the monetization **privacy lever**, see [[monetization-plan]]). Start corpus = the user's own growing match collection + ShuttleSet.
- **Rally `[start,end)` depth** (cheapest; extensible later to shot-counts/(x,y)).
- **Tiered turnaround:** slow human tier (authoritative) + fast automated tier (CI / "surprise regression"). ⚠️ **Oracle rule:** the fast-tier auto-labeler MUST be a different model lineage than production, else shared blind spots → false green.
- **Vendor bake-off**, favouring **Bangalore/India-local** (user is Bangalore-based).

**Architecture (specced, NOT yet built):** an `AnnotationProvider` registry (same pattern as the segmenter/provider registries) with `FileProvider` + `HumanVendorProvider` + `AutomatedProvider`, and a thin `regress(video)` wrapper over the existing eval harness. Implementation is **deferred until the user picks vendors offline.**

**Vendor leads (verified India presence + video; pricing NOT verified — all quote-only):** primary = **iMerit** (Bengaluru office, sports domain, enterprise QA) and **Taskmonk** (Bengaluru, platform+service, likely cheaper/agile); heavyweight third = **TELUS/Playment** (Bangalore-founded CV). Excluded: **Karya** (Bengaluru but speech/text only, no video). Tier-2 leads + confidence labels in the doc. (PR #12.)

1	from typing import Dict, Type
2	from backend.providers.base import AIVideoProvider
3	from backend.providers.gemini import GeminiProvider
4	
5	class ProviderRegistry:
6	    def __init__(self):
7	        self._registry: Dict[str, Type[AIVideoProvider]] = {}
8	        # Register Gemini provider
9	        self.register("gemini", GeminiProvider)
10	
11	    def register(self, name: str, provider_cls: Type[AIVideoProvider]):
12	        self._registry[name] = provider_cls
13	
14	    def get(self, name: str, api_key: str, model_name: str) -> AIVideoProvider:
15	        if name not in self._registry:
16	            raise KeyError(f"AI Provider '{name}' is not registered in the ProviderRegistry. Available: {list(self._registry.keys())}")
17	        return self._registry[name](api_key=api_key, model_name=model_name)
18	
19	    def list_available(self) -> Dict[str, str]:
20	        return {name: cls.__doc__ or f"{name.capitalize()} provider" for name, cls in self._registry.items()}
21	
22	provider_registry = ProviderRegistry()

### 385. user (2026-05-30T15:35:05.002Z)

1	"""CLI to evaluate detected rallies against hand-annotated ground truth.
2	
3	Scores predictions already stored in the SQLite DB (run the pipeline first) — no
4	API calls, fully deterministic. See docs/EVALUATION.md for the workflow.
5	
6	Examples
7	--------
8	Create an empty annotation file to fill in:
9	    python run_eval.py --scaffold --annotations rallies.csv
10	
11	Score a video's detected rallies (identify it by file or by stored video_id):
12	    python run_eval.py --video "C:/path/match.mp4" --annotations rallies.csv
13	    python run_eval.py --video-id <md5> --annotations rallies.csv --stage both --iou 0.5
14	
15	Show which rallies were missed / spurious, and dump a JSON report:
16	    python run_eval.py --video-id <md5> --annotations rallies.csv --show-failures --json report.json
17	"""
18	
19	import os
20	import sys
21	import json
22	import hashlib
23	import argparse
24	import logging
25	
26	from backend.database import Database
27	from backend.eval.annotations import load_annotations, write_scaffold
28	from backend.eval.harness import evaluate, format_report_text, report_to_dict, STAGES
29	
30	
31	def get_file_md5(filepath: str) -> str:
32	    """MD5 of a file, chunked — matches how the pipeline derives video_id."""
33	    hasher = hashlib.md5()
34	    with open(filepath, "rb") as f:
35	        for chunk in iter(lambda: f.read(8192), b""):
36	            hasher.update(chunk)
37	    return hasher.hexdigest()
38	
39	
40	def _default_db_path() -> str:
41	    """Resolve the DB path from config.json (output.db_name) under output/."""
42	    db_name = "sports_indexer.db"
43	    if os.path.exists("config.json"):
44	        try:
45	            with open("config.json") as f:
46	                db_name = json.load(f).get("output", {}).get("db_name", db_name)
47	        except (json.JSONDecodeError, OSError):
48	            pass
49	    return os.path.join("output", db_name)
50	
51	
52	_EPILOG = """\
53	Prerequisite:
54	  The harness scores predictions read from the SQLite DB — it does NOT run the
55	  pipeline. So you must first PROCESS the video (e.g. via run_process_and_stitch.py)
56	  so its detections land in output/sports_indexer.db. Then point this tool at the
57	  same video file (--video) or its stored MD5 (--video-id). If you get
58	  "database not found" or zero predictions, the video hasn't been processed yet.
59	
60	See docs/EVALUATION.md for the annotation format and how to read the output.
61	"""
62	
63	
64	def build_parser() -> argparse.ArgumentParser:
65	    p = argparse.ArgumentParser(
66	        description="Evaluate detected rallies vs ground-truth annotations.",
67	        epilog=_EPILOG,
68	        formatter_class=argparse.RawDescriptionHelpFormatter,
69	    )
70	    p.add_argument("--annotations", help="Path to the ground-truth rally CSV (start,end per row).")
71	    p.add_argument("--video", help="Path to the video file (its MD5 becomes the video_id).")
72	    p.add_argument("--video-id", help="Stored video_id (MD5) to evaluate, instead of --video.")
73	    p.add_argument("--db", default=None, help="SQLite DB path (default: from config.json).")
74	    p.add_argument("--stage", choices=("candidates", "final", "both"), default="both",
75	                   help="Which prediction stage(s) to score (default: both).")
76	    p.add_argument("--detector", default=None,
77	                   help="Only score candidates from this detector (e.g. yolo_hybrid, tracknet_hybrid).")
78	    p.add_argument("--iou", type=float, default=0.5, help="IoU match threshold (default: 0.5).")
79	    p.add_argument("--pr-curve", action="store_true", help="Include an IoU sweep (0.1..0.9) in the report.")
80	    p.add_argument("--show-failures", action="store_true",
81	                   help="List missed (FN) and spurious (FP) rallies with timestamps.")
82	    p.add_argument("--json", dest="json_out", default=None, help="Write a JSON report to this path.")
83	    p.add_argument("--scaffold", action="store_true",
84	                   help="Write an empty annotation CSV to --annotations and exit.")
85	    return p
86	
87	
88	def main(argv=None) -> int:
89	    logging.basicConfig(level=logging.INFO, format="%(levelname)s %(message)s")
90	    args = build_parser().parse_args(argv)
91	
92	    # Scaffold mode: just write the template and exit.
93	    if args.scaffold:
94	        if not args.annotations:
95	            print("ERROR: --scaffold requires --annotations <path>", file=sys.stderr)
96	            return 2
97	        write_scaffold(args.annotations)
98	        print(f"Wrote empty annotation file: {args.annotations}")
99	        return 0
100	
101	    if not args.annotations:
102	        print("ERROR: --annotations is required (or use --scaffold).", file=sys.stderr)
103	        return 2
104	
105	    # Resolve the video_id.
106	    if args.video_id:
107	        video_id = args.video_id
108	    elif args.video:
109	        if not os.path.exists(args.video):
110	            print(f"ERROR: video not found: {args.video}", file=sys.stderr)
111	            return 2
112	        print("Computing video MD5...")
113	        video_id = get_file_md5(args.video)
114	        print(f"video_id = {video_id}")
115	    else:
116	        print("ERROR: provide --video <path> or --video-id <md5>.", file=sys.stderr)
117	        return 2
118	
119	    db_path = args.db or _default_db_path()
120	    if not os.path.exists(db_path):
121	        print(f"ERROR: database not found at {db_path}. Run the pipeline first.", file=sys.stderr)
122	        return 2
123	
124	    gt = load_annotations(args.annotations)
125	    if not gt:
126	        print(f"WARNING: no ground-truth rallies in {args.annotations}.", file=sys.stderr)
127	
128	    stages = list(STAGES) if args.stage == "both" else [args.stage]
129	    db = Database(db_path)
130	    report = evaluate(db, video_id, gt, stages, iou_threshold=args.iou,
131	                      detector=args.detector, with_pr_curve=args.pr_curve)
132	
133	    print(format_report_text(report, show_failures=args.show_failures))
134	
135	    if args.json_out:
136	        with open(args.json_out, "w") as f:
137	            json.dump(report_to_dict(report), f, indent=2)
138	        print(f"\nJSON report written to {args.json_out}")
139	
140	    return 0
141	
142	
143	if __name__ == "__main__":
144	    raise SystemExit(main())
145

### 386. user (2026-05-30T15:35:05.971Z)

1	import time
2	import json
3	import logging
4	from typing import Tuple
5	from google import genai
6	from google.genai import types
7	from backend.providers.base import AIVideoProvider
8	
9	logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)
10	
11	class GeminiProvider(AIVideoProvider):
12	    def __init__(self, api_key: str, model_name: str):
13	        super().__init__(api_key, model_name)
14	        # Initialize client lazily to avoid connection/auth issues during class instantiation
15	        self._client = None
16	
17	    @property
18	    def client(self):
19	        if self._client is None:
20	            self._client = genai.Client(api_key=self.api_key)
21	        return self._client
22	
23	    def analyze_video(self, video_path: str, prompt: str,
24	                      chunk_duration: float = 0.0,
25	                      label: str = "") -> Tuple[list, dict]:
26	        log_prefix = f"Gemini {label}" if label else "Gemini"
27	
28	        metrics = {
29	            "upload_time": 0.0,
30	            "process_time": 0.0,
31	            "gen_time": 0.0
32	        }
33	
34	        # 1. Upload
35	        logger.info(f"[{log_prefix}] Uploading {video_path}...")
36	        t_upload_start = time.time()
37	        try:
38	            video_file = self.client.files.upload(file=video_path)
39	            metrics["upload_time"] = time.time() - t_upload_start
40	            logger.info(f"[{log_prefix}] Upload complete in {metrics['upload_time']:.1f}s. URI: {video_file.uri}")
41	        except Exception as e:
42	            logger.error(f"[{log_prefix}] Upload failed: {e}")
43	            return [], metrics
44	
45	        # 2. Wait for processing on Google servers
46	        t_process_start = time.time()
47	        try:
48	            while video_file.state.name == "PROCESSING":
49	                logger.info(f"[{log_prefix}] Processing on Google servers. Elapsed: {time.time()-t_process_start:.1f}s. Retrying in 10s...")
50	                time.sleep(10)
51	                video_file = self.client.files.get(name=video_file.name)
52	            if video_file.state.name == "FAILED":
53	                logger.error(f"[{log_prefix}] Video processing failed: {video_file.error.message}")
54	                try:
55	                    self.client.files.delete(name=video_file.name)
56	                except Exception:
57	                    pass
58	                return [], metrics
59	        except Exception as e:
60	            logger.error(f"[{log_prefix}] Status check failed: {e}")
61	            try:
62	                self.client.files.delete(name=video_file.name)
63	            except Exception:
64	                pass
65	            return [], metrics
66	
67	        metrics["process_time"] = time.time() - t_process_start
68	        logger.info(f"[{log_prefix}] Processing finished in {metrics['process_time']:.1f}s.")
69	
70	        # 3. Call Gemini
71	        logger.info(f"[{log_prefix}] Submitting multimodal request to {self.model_name}...")
72	        t_gen_start = time.time()
73	        raw_text = None
74	        try:
75	            response = self.client.models.generate_content(
76	                model=self.model_name,
77	                contents=[video_file, prompt],
78	                config=types.GenerateContentConfig(
79	                    response_mime_type="application/json",
80	                    temperature=0.0
81	                )
82	            )
83	            raw_text = response.text
84	            metrics["gen_time"] = time.time() - t_gen_start
85	            logger.info(f"[{log_prefix}] Response received in {metrics['gen_time']:.1f}s:\n{raw_text}")
86	        except Exception as e:
87	            logger.error(f"[{log_prefix}] API call failed: {e}")
88	        finally:
89	            try:
90	                self.client.files.delete(name=video_file.name)
91	            except Exception:
92	                pass
93	
94	        if not raw_text:
95	            return [], metrics
96	
97	        # 4. Parse JSON
98	        try:
99	            segments = json.loads(raw_text)
100	            if not isinstance(segments, list):
101	                logger.error(f"[{log_prefix}] Response is not a JSON array.")
102	                return [], metrics
103	            return segments, metrics
104	        except Exception as e:
105	            logger.error(f"[{log_prefix}] JSON parse failed: {e}")
106	            return [], metrics
107
